A
Overview of the Appendix

The Appendix is organized as follows: Appendix
B
presents the proofs of both Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
for continuous outcome LAGO GLM with independent errors and a general link function. Appendix
C
describes the proofs of both Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
for LAGO GLM with independent errors and a log link function. Appendix
D
extends the LAGO theory to include more than two stages. Appendix
E
explains the motivation for the cubic cost function, and provides a complete set of simulation results for the ones presented in Section
7
of the main text.

B
Proofs of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
with general link function.

B.1
Proof of Theorem
1
for LAGO GLM with general link function: consistency of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

First, the estimating equations from (
3
) of the main text are:

\displaystyle 0=\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

(9)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}E\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}\mid\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-E\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}\mid\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}E\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}\mid\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}-E\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}\mid\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\bigg].

Recall that
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
is the true value of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

(10)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\alpha_{j2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right],

where

g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})=E\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2\right)}\mid\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(2\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}\right).

To show consistency of the estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, we show that in spite of the fact that equation (
9
) does not consist of i.i.d. terms, Theorem
5.9
of
Van der Vaart 2000
can be used. We show that its two conditions are satisfied. First, we show

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\|\xrightarrow{P}0.

(11)

From equation (
9
) and equation (
10
), it follows that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

(12)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\pm\;g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\alpha_{j2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=G_{1,n}^{(g)}+G_{2,n}^{(g)}+G_{3,n}^{(g)}+G_{4,n}^{(g)}+G_{5,n}^{(g)},

where

\displaystyle G_{1,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right],

(13)

\displaystyle G_{2,n}^{(g)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right],

(14)

\displaystyle G_{3,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right.

(15)

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right],

\displaystyle G_{4,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right.

(16)

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right],

\displaystyle G_{5,n}^{(g)}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\left[\left(\alpha_{j1}-\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

(17)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\left[\left(\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}-\alpha_{j1}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\left[\left(\alpha_{j2}-\frac{n_{j}^{(2)}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{n_{j}^{(2)}}{n}-\alpha_{j2}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right].

The five terms
G_{1,n}^{(g)}
,
G_{2,n}^{(g)}
,
G_{3,n}^{(g)}
,
G_{4,n}^{(g)}
, and
G_{5,n}^{(g)}
will be discussed separately. We show the supremum over
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of each term converges to 0 in probability, then the triangle inequality implies equation (
11
).

For
G_{1,n}^{(g)}
from equation (
13
),
we show that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{1,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
by using the concept of Donsker classes from empirical process theory.
Let
O^{(1)}_{ij}=(Y^{(1)}_{ij},\boldsymbol{a}^{(1)}_{j},\boldsymbol{z}^{(1)}_{j})
be the observed data for patient
i
from center
j
in stage 1, and let

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right).

We show that the class of functions
\mathcal{F}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is Donsker by showing that

\left\|\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right\|\leq m(O^{(1)}_{ij})\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right\|\quad\text{ for every }\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},

(18)

for some measurable function
m
with
E\left(m^{2}\right)<\infty
; that
\mathcal{F}
is Donsker then follows from Example 19.7 in
Van der Vaart 2000
. By applying the Mean Value Theorem to each row of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
separately, we find that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})

\displaystyle=\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right)\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right),

where for each row of
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
may take a different value between
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
.
By Assumption
1
and
4
from the main text,
g()
and
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
are continuously differentiable and both
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
O^{(1)}_{ij}
take values in a compact space, each element in
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
is bounded. It follows that

\displaystyle\left\|\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right)(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}^{(g)}\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right\|,

where
C_{1}^{(g)}
is a constant. Equation (
18
) follows, so the class of functions

\mathcal{F}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is Donsker. By Theorem 19.4 (
Van der Vaart 2000
),
\mathcal{F}
is also Glivenko-Cantelli. Since for each
\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
E\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right)=0
, then

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)-E\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right)\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

Notice that the
O_{ij}^{(1)}
are i.i.d. for each
j
separately,
{n_{j}^{(1)}}/{n}<1
, and
j
is finite, so

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right\|\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right\|.

We conclude that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{1,n}^{(g)}\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0.

For the term
G_{2,n}^{(g)}
from equation (
14
), let
Y_{ij}^{(2)}
be the (counterfactual) outcomes under
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
and let
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}
be the corresponding errors that patient
i
in center
j
would have experienced under intervention
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
. We derive

\displaystyle G_{2,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right]

By Assumption
5
from the main text, replacing
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
by the new error terms
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}
does not change the distribution of
G_{2,n}^{(g)}
, so it suffices to show that for

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2,n}^{(g)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right],

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\widetilde{G}_{2,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\pm\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}+\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)},

where

\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right],

\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}.

(19)

We show that both
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
. By the triangle inequality, then
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\widetilde{G}_{2,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Let
O^{(2)}_{ij}=(Y^{(2)}_{ij},\boldsymbol{a}^{(2)}_{j},\boldsymbol{z}^{(2)}_{j})
be the counterfactual data for patient
i
from center
j
in stage 2 under
\boldsymbol{a}^{(2)}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{z}^{(2)}_{j}
and let

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(2)}(O^{(2)}_{ij})=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}.

Following the same argument as for
G_{1,n}^{(g)}
, for fixed value of
j
, the class of functions
\mathcal{F}_{2}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is a Donsker class and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.

For each of the
P
components of
\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
from equation (
19
), by the Mean Value Theorem, we derive

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,\;p}^{(g)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left[\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{p}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{jp}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{jp}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{jp}^{(2)}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\}.

By Assumption
4
from the main text and Lemma
B.1
from the Appendix, it follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\max_{j}{\left\|\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{p}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{jp}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right\|}\max_{j}\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{jp}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{jp}^{(2)}\right)\right\|\sup_{ij}{\left\|\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\|}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

This argument can be applied to all
P
components of
\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
, so
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.
Hence
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{2,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Consider
G_{3,n}^{(g)}
from equation (
15
). By the Mean Value Theorem, the supremum over
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of each of the
P
components of
G_{3,n}^{(g)}
is

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\right]\rule{0.0pt}{22.76228pt}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{p}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{jp}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right]\right.\right.

\displaystyle\Biggl.\Biggl.\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{jp}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{jp}^{(2)}\right)\Biggr\}\Biggr\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\max_{j}{\left\|\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a_{p}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{jp}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}_{p}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right\|}

\displaystyle\max_{j}\biggr\|\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{jp}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{jp}^{(2)}\right)\biggr\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

The convergence to 0 follows from Assumption
4
of the main text and Lemma
B.1
of the Appendix, so
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{3,n}^{(g)}\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0
. By applying the same argument to
G_{4,n}^{(g)}
(equation (
16
)), we conclude that also
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{4,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Consider the first term of
G_{5,n}^{(g)}
(equation (
17
)), by Assumption
4
from the main text and
\alpha_{jk}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n_{j}^{(k)}/n
, for the finite many
j
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\left[\left(\alpha_{j1}-\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\max_{j}\left\|\left(\alpha_{j1}-\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}

\displaystyle 0.

By applying the same argument to the other terms of
G_{5,n}^{(g)}
and the triangle inequality,
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{5,n}^{(g)}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{1,n}^{(g)}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{2,n}^{(g)}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{3,n}^{(g)}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{4,n}^{(g)}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{5,n}^{(g)}\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

The second condition in Theorem 5.9 of
Van der Vaart 2000
is

\inf_{\boldsymbol{\beta}:\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right\|>0}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\|>0=\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right)\right\|.

Equation (
10
) implies that
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right)\right\|=0
. Furthermore,
\boldsymbol{u}^{(g)}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)
is the same as for a fixed two-stage design with
a_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,a_{J^{(1)}}^{(1)},a_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,a_{J^{(2)}}^{(2)}
as interventions decided on before the trial,
so regular GEE theory applies here. In order for LAGO to work properly, we need variations in the intervention components to identify the treatment effect parameter. The uniqueness of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
as a maximizer or zero has been studied by various authors, see e.g. Chapter 2.2 of
Fahrmeir and
Tutz 2013
. Thus, provided there is enough variation in the intervention, the second condition in Theorem 5.9 of
Van der Vaart 2000
is often also satisfied and we conclude that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is consistent.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
2
for LAGO GLM with general link function: asymptotic normality of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

To prove asymptotic normality of the final estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, we first prove

Lemma B.1
.

Under Assumption
3
,
there exist
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
, which is the probability limit of
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
as
n^{(1)}\rightarrow\infty
such that for stage
2
,
\max_{j=1,\cdots,J^{(2)}}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.

By Assumption
3
and the Continuous Mapping Theorem,
for each
j
,
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\xrightarrow{P}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
. Since the number of centers in stage
2
is fixed, taking the maximum over all
j
is a continuous operation. Lemma
B.1
follows by the Continuous Mapping Theorem.
∎

Under Assumptions
1

-

5
, and Lemma
B.1
, we show that

{\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\xrightarrow{D}N(0,J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)^{-1}V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)^{-1}),}

(20)

where the explicit forms of
J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)
and
V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)
are
given in Theorem
2
.
The corresponding estimators are

\displaystyle\hat{J}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\frac{n_{j}^{(2)}}{n}\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2},

(21)

\displaystyle\hat{V}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\left\{\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)^{2}\right\}

(22)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\left\{\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)^{2}\right\}.

Applying the Mean Value Theorem to each component of
\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
from equation (
3
),

{0=\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})^{T},}

where for each row of
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
takes a possibly row-dependent value on the line between
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
. It follows that

{\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)=-\sqrt{n}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right).}

(23)

We first show that
\left(-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right)\right)
converges in probability to
J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)
. Then we show that

\sqrt{n}\;\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)
converges to a normal distribution with mean 0 and variance
V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta^{*}}\right)
. Equation (
20
) then follows from Slutsky’s Theorem.

Because of equation (
3
) of the main text,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

(24)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=G_{6,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}),

where

\displaystyle G_{6,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)Y_{ij}^{(1)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\},

\displaystyle G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right.

\displaystyle-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}.

Let

\displaystyle G_{6,n}^{(g)*}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\},

\displaystyle G_{7,n}^{(g)*}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}.

We show that
\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\left(G_{6,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)+G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)
converges in probability to 0. First,

{\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{{\beta}}})-\left(G_{6,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)+G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)=\left(G_{6,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{6,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)+\left(G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right).}

We show that both terms converge in probability to 0.
For the first term, using
Y_{ij}^{(1)}=g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})+\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}
,

\displaystyle G_{6,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{6,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)

(25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left\{\left(\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{2}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)\;\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}\right.+\left(\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle-\left(\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})

\displaystyle+\left.\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}-\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}.

Notice that the
pq
-th entry of the first term of equation (
25
)
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\left\{\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{p}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{p}}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{q}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{q}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right.

(26)

\displaystyle-\left.\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{p}}\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{p}}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{q}}\right|_{{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}_{q}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right\}.

Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
and therefore
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is consistent,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\xrightarrow{P}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
. By Assumption
4
and the Continuous Mapping Theorem, equation (
26
) converges in probability to 0 for each entry. Since
J^{(1)}
is fixed,

g()
is in
C^{2}
,
and
{n_{j}^{(1)}}/{n}\leq 1
, we conclude that equation (
25
) goes to 0 in probability.
Next, following the same arguments as for
{G_{1,n}^{(g)}}
in the proof of consistency (Appendix
B.1
),

{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{2}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\left(1\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(1\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)\;\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}\xrightarrow{P}0.}

We conclude that indeed
G_{6,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{6,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

For
G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)
,
we first show
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{7,n}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0
, after which we show that
\left(G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)*}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)\xrightarrow{P}0.

Using that
Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}=g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}})+\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
, from equation (
24
)

\displaystyle G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=G_{7\_1,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+G_{7\_2,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+G_{7\_3,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+G_{7\_4,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

where

{G_{7\_1,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right\},}

\displaystyle G_{7\_2,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right\},

\displaystyle G_{7\_3,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}^{2}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\},

\displaystyle G_{7\_4,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}-\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}.

Following the same arguments as for
G_{2,n}^{(g)}
,
G_{3,n}^{(g)}
, and
G_{4,n}^{(g)}
in the proof of consistency (Appendix
B.1
), respectively, we conclude that suprema over
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of
G_{7\_1,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
,
G_{7\_2,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
,
G_{7\_3,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
and
G_{7\_4,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})
converge to 0 in probability. Next, notice that

\displaystyle G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})

\displaystyle=\left(G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\right)+\left(G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right).

Since
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
, it follows that
G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0.
Similar to equation (
25
), we apply the Mean Value Theorem to conclude that
G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
. Combining, we conclude
G_{7,n}^{(g)}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}})-G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

It follows that
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{{\beta}}})-\left(G_{6,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)\xrightarrow{P}0
. Thus, calculating the limit of
-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{{\beta}}})
is equivalent to calculating the limit of
-\left(G_{6,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})+G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)
. We therefore show that
-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{{\beta}}})
converges in probability to
J(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
from Theorem
2
.

\displaystyle G_{6,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left\{-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left\{-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left\{\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\left\{-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left\{-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

Similarly,

{G_{7,n}^{(g)^{*}}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})-\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j2}\left\{-\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}\xrightarrow{P}0.}

We conclude that

{\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}({\boldsymbol{{\beta}}})\xrightarrow{P}J(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}).}

(27)

To show that
\sqrt{n}\>\>\boldsymbol{U}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)
from equation (
23
) converges in distribution to
N\left(0,V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)
, we derive

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\ \boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})

(28)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}-g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{\left(2\right)};\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\pm\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\frac{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(1)}}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(1)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\frac{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(2)}}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(2)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}+\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}+\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)},

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\frac{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(1)}}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(1)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)},

(29)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\frac{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(2)}}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(2)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}.

In order to demonstrate that
\sqrt{n}\ \boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
converges in distribution to a normal distribution, we first show that

\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}
converges in probability to 0.
Subsequently, we show that the joint distribution of
\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
converges in distribution to a normal distribution.
This method works as under Assumption
5
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
are independent.
Therefore, any dependence between stages only exists in
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}
, which itself converges to 0 in probability.

By Assumption
5
, for each fixed value of
j
, the central limit theorem for i.i.d. observations implies that

{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(1)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}}

converges in distribution to a normal distribution with mean 0 and variance

{\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}).}

From e.g. Lévy’s Continuity Theorem for characteristic functions
(
Eisenberg and
Gan 1983
)
and Slutsky’s Theorem, it follows that
\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}
converges to a normal distribution with mean 0 and variance

{\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\alpha_{j1}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}).}

We show
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}\xrightarrow{P}0
by showing that
E(\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)})=0
and
E({\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}}^{\otimes 2})\xrightarrow{}0
, so that
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}\xrightarrow{P}0
by Chebyshev’s Inequality. We show
E(\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)})=0
first. For each
j
, we have

\displaystyle E\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right\}=0

by conditioning on
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
, so
E(\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)})=0
. To see that
E({\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}}^{\otimes 2})\xrightarrow{}0
,

\displaystyle E\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}^{(2)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right\}^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}E\left\{\left[\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right]^{\otimes 2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}E\left\{\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\left(\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right)^{2}\right\}.

where the second line follows since all terms belonging to different
i
are uncorrelated, which can be seen by conditioning on
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}
.

From Assumption
5
, we have
E\left(\left.\left(\epsilon_{ij}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}\right)^{2}\right|\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}\right)=\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})
.
Thus, by conditioning on
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{\left(2,n^{(1)}\right)}
,

\displaystyle E\left({\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}}^{\otimes 2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})\frac{n_{j}^{(2)}}{n}E\left\{\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})-\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\right\}.

By Lemma
B.1
, the Continuous Mapping Theorem and Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem,

E({\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}}^{\otimes 2})\xrightarrow{}0
. By Chebyshev’s Inequality,
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_1,n}^{(g)}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

For
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
,
by Assumption
5
replacing
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
by the error terms
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}
under the limiting intervention
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
does not change the distribution of
\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}
, so it suffices to show that

{{\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)^{*}}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}}

converges to a normal distribution. Following the same argument as for
\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}
,
{\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)\;*}}
converges to a normal distribution with mean 0 and variance

{\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\alpha_{j2}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)^{\otimes 2}\sigma^{2}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}).}

Hence,
\sqrt{n}\ \boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
has the same asymptotic distribution as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}+{\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)\;*}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(1)}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}}g^{-1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)};\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}.

Since
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
are fixed,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1,n}^{(g)}
and
{\boldsymbol{U}_{2\_2,n}^{(g)}}
are independent of one another. Thus, equation (
28
) has the same asymptotic distribution as a fixed two-stage design with
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,\boldsymbol{a}_{J^{(1)}}^{(1)},\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,\boldsymbol{a}_{J^{(2)}}^{(2)}
as interventions decided on before the trial.
The limiting distribution of
\sqrt{n}\ \boldsymbol{U}^{(g)}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*})
is
N(0,V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right))

(
Liang and Zeger 1986
)
.

Combining this with equation (
27
) implies that Theorem
2
holds.

C
Proofs of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
with log link function.

In this section, we present specific illustrations of the proofs of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
from the main text using the log link function for clarity. While these specific illustrations are not as general as the original proofs, they serve to demonstrate the concepts in a more concrete manner.

The five terms
G_{1,n}
,
G_{2,n}
,
G_{3,n}
,
G_{4,n}
, and
G_{5,n}
, will be discussed separately. We first show the supremum over
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of each term converges to 0 in probability, then the triangle inequality implies equation (
32
).

For
G_{1,n}
from equation (
34
), we show that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|G_{1,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
by using the concept of Donsker classes from empirical process theory. Let
O^{(1)}_{ij}=(Y^{(1)}_{ij},\boldsymbol{a}^{(1)}_{j},\boldsymbol{z}^{(1)}_{j})
be the observed data for patient
i
from center
j
in stage 1 and let

{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})=exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)\left(Y_{ij}^{(1)}-exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right).}

We show that the class of functions
\mathcal{F}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is Donsker
by showing that

\left\|\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right\|\leq m(O^{(1)}_{ij})\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right\|\quad\text{ for every }\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},

(39)

for some measurable function
m
with
E\left(m^{2}\right)<\infty
; that
\mathcal{F}
is Donsker then follows from Example 19.7 in
Van der Vaart 2000
. By applying the mean value theorem to each row of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
separately, we find that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})

\displaystyle=\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right)(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}),

where for each row of
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
may take a different value between
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}
.
By Assumption
1
and
4
from the main text,
g()
and
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
are continuously differentiable and both
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
O^{(1)}_{ij}
take values in a compact space, each element in
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})
is bounded. It follows that

\displaystyle\left\|\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\left(\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\boldsymbol{\beta}}\right|_{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}(O^{(1)}_{ij})\right)(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right\|

where
C_{1}
is a constant. Equation (
39
) follows, so the class of functions
\mathcal{F}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is Donsker. By Theorem 19.4 in
Van der Vaart 2000
,
\mathcal{F}
is also Glivenko-Cantelli. Since for each
\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
E\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right)=0
, then

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)-E\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right)\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0.

Notice that the
O_{ij}^{(1)}
are iid for each
j
separately,
\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}<1
, and
j
is finite, so

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right\|\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n_{j}^{(1)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(1)}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left(O_{ij}^{(1)}\right)\right\|.

We conclude that
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{1,n}^{(g)}\right\|\xrightarrow{P}0.

For the term
G_{2,n}
from equation (
35
), let
Y_{ij}^{(2)}
be the (counterfactual) outcomes under
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
.
and let
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}
be the corresponding errors that patient
i
in center
j
would have experienced under intervention
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}
.
We derive

\displaystyle G_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left(Y_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\right]

By Assumption
5
from the main text, replacing
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
by the new error terms
\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}
does not change the distribution of
G_{2,n}
, so it suffices to show that for

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n_{1})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n_{1})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right],

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n_{1})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n_{1})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\pm exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}+\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n},

where

{\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right],}

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[\left(exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\right.\right.

(40)

\displaystyle\left.\left.-exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right].

We show that both
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_1,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
. By the triangle inequality, then
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.

Let
O^{(2)}_{ij}=(Y^{(2)}_{ij},\boldsymbol{a}^{(2)}_{j},\boldsymbol{z}^{(2)}_{j})
be the counterfactual data for patient
i
from center
j
in stage 2 under
\boldsymbol{a}^{(2)}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{z}^{(2)}_{j}
and let

{\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(2)}(O^{(2)}_{ij})=\left(exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}.}

Following the same argument as for
G_{1,n}
, the class of functions
\mathcal{F}_{2}=\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{(2)}:\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{B}}\right)
is a Donsker class and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|{G}_{2\_1,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
.

For the term
{G}_{2\_2,n}
from equation (
40
), consider the most complicated entry with
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}
. By the Mean Value Theorem, we derive

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left[e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right]\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left[\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}a+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\>a\>\right]\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left\{\left(\left(1+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\right)\left(\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\},

By Assumption
4
from the main text and Lemma
B.1
from the Appendix, it follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}{\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}}-e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right]\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\left(1+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\right)e^{(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)})}\right\|\max_{j}{\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right\|}\sup_{ij}\left\|\epsilon_{ij}^{(2)}\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

This argument can be applied to other entries of
\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}
, so
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|\widetilde{G}_{2\_2,n}\|\xrightarrow{P}0
. Hence,
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{2,n}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0.

Consider
G_{3,n}
from equation (
36
). By the Mean Value Theorem, the supremum over
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of the first entry is

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\Biggl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[e^{\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}e^{\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-e^{\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}e^{\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}\right]\Biggr\|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\Biggl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(2)}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}^{(2)}}\left[e^{\left((\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{*})+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*})\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*})\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-e^{\left((\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{*})+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*})\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*})\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)}\right]\Biggr\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\right|_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{a}}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\beta})}exp\left((\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{*})+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*})a+(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*})\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\max_{j}\left\|\boldsymbol{A}_{j}^{(2,n^{(1)})}-\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(2)}\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

The convergence to 0 follows from Assumption
4
of the main text and Lemma
B.1
of the Appendix.
The same argument can be applied to other entries of
G_{3,n}
from equation (
36
), so
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{3,n}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
. By applying the same argument to each entry of
G_{4,n}
from equation (
37
), we conclude that also
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{4,n}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Consider the first term of
G_{5,n}
(equation (
38
)), by Assumption
4
from the main text and
\alpha_{jk}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}n_{j}^{(k)}/n
, for the finitely many
j
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{J^{(1)}}\left[\left(\alpha_{j1}-\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\right)exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\max_{j}\left\|\left(\alpha_{j1}-\frac{n_{j}^{(1)}}{n}\right)exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)exp\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)^{T}\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

By applying the same argument to the other terms of
G_{5,n}
and the triangle inequality,

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{5,n}\right\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|\boldsymbol{U}(\boldsymbol{\beta})-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{1,n}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{2,n}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{3,n}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{4,n}\right\|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\left\|G_{5,n}\right\|

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}0.

The second condition in Theorem 5.9 of
Van der Vaart 2000
is

\inf_{\boldsymbol{\beta}:\left\|\boldsymbol{\beta}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right\|>0}\|\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\|>0=\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right)\right\|.

Equation (
31
) implies that
\left\|\boldsymbol{u}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}\right)\right\|=0
. Furthermore,
\boldsymbol{u}\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}\right)
is the same as for a fixed two-stage design with
a_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,a_{J^{(1)}}^{(1)},a_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,a_{J^{(2)}}^{(2)}
as interventions decided on before the trial,
so regular GEE theory applies here. In order for LAGO to work properly, we need variations in the intervention components to identify the treatment effect parameter. The uniqueness of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}
as a maximizer or zero has been studied by various authors, see e.g. Chapter 2.2 of
Fahrmeir and
Tutz 2013
. Thus, provide there is enough variation in the intervention, the second condition in Theorem 5.9 of
Van der Vaart 2000
is often also satisfied and we conclude that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
is consistent.

C.2
Proof of Theorem 2 for LAGO GLM with log link function: asymptotic normality of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

Under Assumptions
1
–
5
from the main text and Lemma
B.1
from the Appendix, we show that

\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)\xrightarrow{D}N(0,J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)^{-1}V\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)J\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{*}\right)^{-1}),

(41)

where
the explicit forms of

and
